     5→# Hermes Agent ⚕
     6→
     7→<p align="center">
     8→  <a href="https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/"><img src="https://img.shields.io/badge/Docs-hermes--agent.nousresearch.com-FFD700?style=for-the-badge" alt="Documentation"></a>
     9→  <a href="https://discord.gg/NousResearch"><img src="https://img.shields.io/badge/Discord-5865F2?style=for-the-badge&logo=discord&logoColor=white" alt="Discord"></a>
    10→  <a href="https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/blob/main/LICENSE"><img src="https://img.shields.io/badge/License-MIT-green?style=for-the-badge" alt="License: MIT"></a>
    11→  <a href="https://nousresearch.com"><img src="https://img.shields.io/badge/Built%20by-Nous%20Research-blueviolet?style=for-the-badge" alt="Built by Nous Research"></a>
    12→</p>
    13→
    14→**The self-improving AI agent built by [Nous Research](https://nousresearch.com).** It's the only agent with a built-in learning loop — it creates skills from experience, improves them during use, nudges itself to persist knowledge, searches its own past conversations, and builds a deepening model of who you are across sessions. Run it on a $5 VPS, a GPU cluster, or serverless infrastructure that costs nearly nothing when idle. It's not tied to your laptop — talk to it from Telegram while it works on a cloud VM.
    15→
    16→Use any model you want — [Nous Portal](https://portal.nousresearch.com), [OpenRouter](https://openrouter.ai) (200+ models), [z.ai/GLM](https://z.ai), [Kimi/Moonshot](https://platform.moonshot.ai), [MiniMax](https://www.minimax.io), OpenAI, or your own endpoint. Switch with `hermes model` — no code changes, no lock-in.
    17→
    18→<table>
    19→<tr><td><b>A real terminal interface</b></td><td>Full TUI with multiline editing, slash-command autocomplete, conversation history, interrupt-and-redirect, and streaming tool output.</td></tr>
    20→<tr><td><b>Lives where you do</b></td><td>Telegram, Discord, Slack, WhatsApp, Signal, and CLI — all from a single gateway process. Voice memo transcription, cross-platform conversation continuity.</td></tr>
    21→<tr><td><b>A closed learning loop</b></td><td>Agent-curated memory with periodic nudges. Autonomous skill creation after complex tasks. Skills self-improve during use. FTS5 session search with LLM summarization for cross-session recall. <a href="https://github.com/plastic-labs/honcho">Honcho</a> dialectic user modeling. Compatible with the <a href="https://agentskills.io">agentskills.io</a> open standard.</td></tr>
    22→<tr><td><b>Scheduled automations</b></td><td>Built-in cron scheduler with delivery to any platform. Daily reports, nightly backups, weekly audits — all in natural language, running unattended.</td></tr>
    23→<tr><td><b>Delegates and parallelizes</b></td><td>Spawn isolated subagents for parallel workstreams. Write Python scripts that call tools via RPC, collapsing multi-step pipelines into zero-context-cost turns.</td></tr>
    24→<tr><td><b>Runs anywhere, not just your laptop</b></td><td>Six terminal backends — local, Docker, SSH, Daytona, Singularity, and Modal. Daytona and Modal offer serverless persistence — your agent's environment hibernates when idle and wakes on demand, costing nearly nothing between sessions. Run it on a $5 VPS or a GPU cluster.</td></tr>
    25→<tr><td><b>Research-ready</b></td><td>Batch trajectory generation, Atropos RL environments, trajectory compression for training the next generation of tool-calling models.</td></tr>
    26→</table>

    28→---
    29→
    30→## Quick Install
    31→
    32→```bash
    33→curl -fsSL https://raw.githubusercontent.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent/main/scripts/install.sh | bash
    34→```
    35→
    36→Works on Linux, macOS, and WSL2. The installer handles everything — Python, Node.js, dependencies, and the `hermes` command. No prerequisites except git.
    37→
    38→> **Windows:** Native Windows is not supported. Please install [WSL2](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/wsl/install) and run the command above.
    39→
    40→After installation:
    41→
    42→```bash
    43→source ~/.bashrc    # reload shell (or: source ~/.zshrc)
    44→hermes              # start chatting!
    45→```
    46→
    47→---
    48→
    49→## Getting Started
    50→
    51→```bash
    52→hermes              # Interactive CLI — start a conversation
    53→hermes model        # Choose your LLM provider and model
    54→hermes tools        # Configure which tools are enabled
    55→hermes config set   # Set individual config values
    56→hermes gateway      # Start the messaging gateway (Telegram, Discord, etc.)
    57→hermes setup        # Run the full setup wizard (configures everything at once)
    58→hermes claw migrate # Migrate from OpenClaw (if coming from OpenClaw)
    59→hermes update       # Update to the latest version
    60→hermes doctor       # Diagnose any issues
    61→```
    62→
    63→📖 **[Full documentation →](https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/)**

    90→
    91→## Migrating from OpenClaw
    92→
    93→If you're coming from OpenClaw, Hermes can automatically import your settings, memories, skills, and API keys.
    94→
    95→**During first-time setup:** The setup wizard (`hermes setup`) automatically detects `~/.openclaw` and offers to migrate before configuration begins.
    96→
    97→**Anytime after install:**
    98→
    99→```bash
   100→hermes claw migrate              # Interactive migration (full preset)
   101→hermes claw migrate --dry-run    # Preview what would be migrated
   102→hermes claw migrate --preset user-data   # Migrate without secrets
   103→hermes claw migrate --overwrite  # Overwrite existing conflicts
   104→```
   105→
   106→What gets imported:
   107→- **SOUL.md** — persona file
   108→- **Memories** — MEMORY.md and USER.md entries
   109→- **Skills** — user-created skills → `~/.hermes/skills/openclaw-imports/`
   110→- **Command allowlist** — approval patterns
   111→- **Messaging settings** — platform configs, allowed users, working directory
   112→- **API keys** — allowlisted secrets (Telegram, OpenRouter, OpenAI, Anthropic, ElevenLabs)
   113→- **TTS assets** — workspace audio files
   114→- **Workspace instructions** — AGENTS.md (with `--workspace-target`)
   115→
   116→See `hermes claw migrate --help` for all options, or use the `openclaw-migration` skill for an interactive agent-guided migration with dry-run previews.
   117→
   118→---

   120→## Contributing
   121→
   122→We welcome contributions! See the [Contributing Guide](https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/developer-guide/contributing) for development setup, code style, and PR process.
   123→
   124→Quick start for contributors:
   125→
   126→```bash
   127→git clone https://github.com/NousResearch/hermes-agent.git
   128→cd hermes-agent
   129→git submodule update --init mini-swe-agent   # required terminal backend
   130→curl -LsSf https://astral.sh/uv/install.sh | sh
   131→uv venv .venv --python 3.11
   132→source .venv/bin/activate
   133→uv pip install -e ".[all,dev]"
   134→uv pip install -e "./mini-swe-agent"
   135→python -m pytest tests/ -q
   136→```
   137→
   138→> **RL Training (optional):** To work on the RL/Tinker-Atropos integration, also run:
   139→> ```bash
   140→> git submodule update --init tinker-atropos
   141→> uv pip install -e "./tinker-atropos"
   142→> ```

1→---
     2→sidebar_position: 8
     3→title: "Security"
     4→description: "Security model, dangerous command approval, user authorization, container isolation, and production deployment best practices"
     5→---
     6→
     7→# Security
     8→
     9→Hermes Agent is designed with a defense-in-depth security model. This page covers every security boundary — from command approval to container isolation to user authorization on messaging platforms.
    10→
    11→## Overview
    12→
    13→The security model has five layers:
    14→
    15→1. **User authorization** — who can talk to the agent (allowlists, DM pairing)
    16→2. **Dangerous command approval** — human-in-the-loop for destructive operations
    17→3. **Container isolation** — Docker/Singularity/Modal sandboxing with hardened settings
    18→4. **MCP credential filtering** — environment variable isolation for MCP subprocesses
    19→5. **Context file scanning** — prompt injection detection in project files
    20→
    21→## Dangerous Command Approval
    22→
    23→Before executing any command, Hermes checks it against a curated list of dangerous patterns. If a match is found, the user must explicitly approve it.
    24→
    25→### What Triggers Approval
    26→
    27→The following patterns trigger approval prompts (defined in `tools/approval.py`):
    28→
    29→| Pattern | Description |
    30→|---------|-------------|
    31→| `rm -r` / `rm --recursive` | Recursive delete |
    32→| `rm ... /` | Delete in root path |
    33→| `chmod 777` | World-writable permissions |
    34→| `mkfs` | Format filesystem |
    35→| `dd if=` | Disk copy |
    36→| `DROP TABLE/DATABASE` | SQL DROP |
    37→| `DELETE FROM` (without WHERE) | SQL DELETE without WHERE |
    38→| `TRUNCATE TABLE` | SQL TRUNCATE |
    39→| `> /etc/` | Overwrite system config |
    40→| `systemctl stop/disable/mask` | Stop/disable system services |
    41→| `kill -9 -1` | Kill all processes |
    42→| `curl ... \| sh` | Pipe remote content to shell |
    43→| `bash -c`, `python -e` | Shell/script execution via flags |
    44→| `find -exec rm`, `find -delete` | Find with destructive actions |
    45→| Fork bomb patterns | Fork bombs |
    46→
    47→:::info
    48→**Container bypass**: When running in `docker`, `singularity`, `modal`, or `daytona` backends, dangerous command checks are **skipped** because the container itself is the security boundary. Destructive commands inside a container can't harm the host.
    49→:::
    50→
    51→### Approval Flow (CLI)
    52→
    53→In the interactive CLI, dangerous commands show an inline approval prompt:
    54→
    55→```
    56→  ⚠️  DANGEROUS COMMAND: recursive delete
    57→      rm -rf /tmp/old-project
    58→
    59→      [o]nce  |  [s]ession  |  [a]lways  |  [d]eny
    60→
    61→      Choice [o/s/a/D]:
    62→```
    63→
    64→The four options:
    65→
    66→- **once** — allow this single execution
    67→- **session** — allow this pattern for the rest of the session
    68→- **always** — add to permanent allowlist (saved to `config.yaml`)
    69→- **deny** (default) — block the command
    70→
    71→### Approval Flow (Gateway/Messaging)
    72→
    73→On messaging platforms, the agent sends the dangerous command details to the chat and waits for the user to reply:
    74→
    75→- Reply **yes**, **y**, **approve**, **ok**, or **go** to approve
    76→- Reply **no**, **n**, **deny**, or **cancel** to deny
    77→
    78→The `HERMES_EXEC_ASK=1` environment variable is automatically set when running the gateway.
    79→
    80→### Permanent Allowlist
    81→
    82→Commands approved with "always" are saved to `~/.hermes/config.yaml`:
    83→
    84→```yaml
    85→# Permanently allowed dangerous command patterns
    86→command_allowlist:
    87→  - rm
    88→  - systemctl
    89→```
    90→
    91→These patterns are loaded at startup and silently approved in all future sessions.
    92→
    93→:::tip
    94→Use `hermes config edit` to review or remove patterns from your permanent allowlist.
    95→:::
    96→
    97→## User Authorization (Gateway)
    98→
    99→When running the messaging gateway, Hermes controls who can interact with the bot through a layered authorization system.
   100→
   101→### Authorization Check Order
   102→
   103→The `_is_user_authorized()` method checks in this order:
   104→
   105→1. **Per-platform allow-all flag** (e.g., `DISCORD_ALLOW_ALL_USERS=true`)
   106→2. **DM pairing approved list** (users approved via pairing codes)
   107→3. **Platform-specific allowlists** (e.g., `TELEGRAM_ALLOWED_USERS=12345,67890`)
   108→4. **Global allowlist** (`GATEWAY_ALLOWED_USERS=12345,67890`)
   109→5. **Global allow-all** (`GATEWAY_ALLOW_ALL_USERS=true`)
   110→6. **Default: deny**
   111→
   112→### Platform Allowlists
   113→
   114→Set allowed user IDs as comma-separated values in `~/.hermes/.env`:
   115→
   116→```bash
   117→# Platform-specific allowlists
   118→TELEGRAM_ALLOWED_USERS=123456789,987654321
   119→DISCORD_ALLOWED_USERS=111222333444555666
   120→WHATSAPP_ALLOWED_USERS=15551234567
   121→SLACK_ALLOWED_USERS=U01ABC123
   122→
   123→# Cross-platform allowlist (checked for all platforms)
   124→GATEWAY_ALLOWED_USERS=123456789
   125→
   126→# Per-platform allow-all (use with caution)
   127→DISCORD_ALLOW_ALL_USERS=true
   128→
   129→# Global allow-all (use with extreme caution)
   130→GATEWAY_ALLOW_ALL_USERS=true
   131→```
   132→
   133→:::warning
   134→If **no allowlists are configured** and `GATEWAY_ALLOW_ALL_USERS` is not set, **all users are denied**. The gateway logs a warning at startup:
   135→
   136→```
   137→No user allowlists configured. All unauthorized users will be denied.
   138→Set GATEWAY_ALLOW_ALL_USERS=true in ~/.hermes/.env to allow open access,
   139→or configure platform allowlists (e.g., TELEGRAM_ALLOWED_USERS=your_id).
   140→```
   141→:::
   142→
   143→### DM Pairing System
   144→
   145→For more flexible authorization, Hermes includes a code-based pairing system. Instead of requiring user IDs upfront, unknown users receive a one-time pairing code that the bot owner approves via the CLI.
   146→
   147→**How it works:**
   148→
   149→1. An unknown user sends a DM to the bot
   150→2. The bot replies with an 8-character pairing code
   151→3. The bot owner runs `hermes pairing approve <platform> <code>` on the CLI
   152→4. The user is permanently approved for that platform
   153→
   154→**Security features** (based on OWASP + NIST SP 800-63-4 guidance):
   155→
   156→| Feature | Details |
   157→|---------|---------|
   158→| Code format | 8-char from 32-char unambiguous alphabet (no 0/O/1/I) |
   159→| Randomness | Cryptographic (`secrets.choice()`) |
   160→| Code TTL | 1 hour expiry |
   161→| Rate limiting | 1 request per user per 10 minutes |
   162→| Pending limit | Max 3 pending codes per platform |
   163→| Lockout | 5 failed approval attempts → 1-hour lockout |
   164→| File security | `chmod 0600` on all pairing data files |
   165→| Logging | Codes are never logged to stdout |
   166→
   167→**Pairing CLI commands:**
   168→
   169→```bash
   170→# List pending and approved users
   171→hermes pairing list
   172→
   173→# Approve a pairing code
   174→hermes pairing approve telegram ABC12DEF
   175→
   176→# Revoke a user's access
   177→hermes pairing revoke telegram 123456789
   178→
   179→# Clear all pending codes
   180→hermes pairing clear-pending
   181→```
   182→
   183→**Storage:** Pairing data is stored in `~/.hermes/pairing/` with per-platform JSON files:
   184→- `{platform}-pending.json` — pending pairing requests
   185→- `{platform}-approved.json` — approved users
   186→- `_rate_limits.json` — rate limit and lockout tracking
   187→
   188→## Container Isolation
   189→
   190→When using the `docker` terminal backend, Hermes applies strict security hardening to every container.
   191→
   192→### Docker Security Flags
   193→
   194→Every container runs with these flags (defined in `tools/environments/docker.py`):
   195→
   196→```python
   197→_SECURITY_ARGS = [
   198→    "--cap-drop", "ALL",                          # Drop ALL Linux capabilities
   199→    "--security-opt", "no-new-privileges",         # Block privilege escalation
   200→    "--pids-limit", "256",                         # Limit process count
   201→    "--tmpfs", "/tmp:rw,nosuid,size=512m",         # Size-limited /tmp
   202→    "--tmpfs", "/var/tmp:rw,noexec,nosuid,size=256m",  # No-exec /var/tmp
   203→    "--tmpfs", "/run:rw,noexec,nosuid,size=64m",   # No-exec /run
   204→]
   205→```
   206→
   207→### Resource Limits
   208→
   209→Container resources are configurable in `~/.hermes/config.yaml`:
   210→
   211→```yaml
   212→terminal:
   213→  backend: docker
   214→  docker_image: "nikolaik/python-nodejs:python3.11-nodejs20"
   215→  container_cpu: 1        # CPU cores
   216→  container_memory: 5120  # MB (default 5GB)
   217→  container_disk: 51200   # MB (default 50GB, requires overlay2 on XFS)
   218→  container_persistent: true  # Persist filesystem across sessions
   219→```
   220→
   221→### Filesystem Persistence
   222→
   223→- **Persistent mode** (`container_persistent: true`): Bind-mounts `/workspace` and `/root` from `~/.hermes/sandboxes/docker/<task_id>/`
   224→- **Ephemeral mode** (`container_persistent: false`): Uses tmpfs for workspace — everything is lost on cleanup
   225→
   226→:::tip
   227→For production gateway deployments, use `docker`, `modal`, or `daytona` backend to isolate agent commands from your host system. This eliminates the need for dangerous command approval entirely.
   228→:::
   229→
   230→## Terminal Backend Security Comparison
   231→
   232→| Backend | Isolation | Dangerous Cmd Check | Best For |
   233→|---------|-----------|-------------------|----------|
   234→| **local** | None — runs on host | ✅ Yes | Development, trusted users |
   235→| **ssh** | Remote machine | ✅ Yes | Running on a separate server |
   236→| **docker** | Container | ❌ Skipped (container is boundary) | Production gateway |
   237→| **singularity** | Container | ❌ Skipped | HPC environments |
   238→| **modal** | Cloud sandbox | ❌ Skipped | Scalable cloud isolation |
   239→| **daytona** | Cloud sandbox | ❌ Skipped | Persistent cloud workspaces |
   240→
   241→## MCP Credential Handling
   242→
   243→MCP (Model Context Protocol) server subprocesses receive a **filtered environment** to prevent accidental credential leakage.
   244→
   245→### Safe Environment Variables
   246→
   247→Only these variables are passed through from the host to MCP stdio subprocesses:
   248→
   249→```
   250→PATH, HOME, USER, LANG, LC_ALL, TERM, SHELL, TMPDIR
   251→```
   252→
   253→Plus any `XDG_*` variables. All other environment variables (API keys, tokens, secrets) are **stripped**.
   254→
   255→Variables explicitly defined in the MCP server's `env` config are passed through:
   256→
   257→```yaml
   258→mcp_servers:
   259→  github:
   260→    command: "npx"
   261→    args: ["-y", "@modelcontextprotocol/server-github"]
   262→    env:
   263→      GITHUB_PERSONAL_ACCESS_TOKEN: "ghp_..."  # Only this is passed
   264→```
   265→
   266→### Credential Redaction
   267→
   268→Error messages from MCP tools are sanitized before being returned to the LLM. The following patterns are replaced with `[REDACTED]`:
   269→
   270→- GitHub PATs (`ghp_...`)
   271→- OpenAI-style keys (`sk-...`)
   272→- Bearer tokens
   273→- `token=`, `key=`, `API_KEY=`, `password=`, `secret=` parameters
   274→
   275→### Context File Injection Protection
   276→
   277→Context files (AGENTS.md, .cursorrules, SOUL.md) are scanned for prompt injection before being included in the system prompt. The scanner checks for:
   278→
   279→- Instructions to ignore/disregard prior instructions
   280→- Hidden HTML comments with suspicious keywords
   281→- Attempts to read secrets (`.env`, `credentials`, `.netrc`)
   282→- Credential exfiltration via `curl`
   283→- Invisible Unicode characters (zero-width spaces, bidirectional overrides)
   284→
   285→Blocked files show a warning:
   286→
   287→```
   288→[BLOCKED: AGENTS.md contained potential prompt injection (prompt_injection). Content not loaded.]
   289→```
   290→
   291→## Best Practices for Production Deployment
   292→
   293→### Gateway Deployment Checklist
   294→
   295→1. **Set explicit allowlists** — never use `GATEWAY_ALLOW_ALL_USERS=true` in production
   296→2. **Use container backend** — set `terminal.backend: docker` in config.yaml
   297→3. **Restrict resource limits** — set appropriate CPU, memory, and disk limits
   298→4. **Store secrets securely** — keep API keys in `~/.hermes/.env` with proper file permissions
   299→5. **Enable DM pairing** — use pairing codes instead of hardcoding user IDs when possible
   300→6. **Review command allowlist** — periodically audit `command_allowlist` in config.yaml
   301→7. **Set `MESSAGING_CWD`** — don't let the agent operate from sensitive directories
   302→8. **Run as non-root** — never run the gateway as root
   303→9. **Monitor logs** — check `~/.hermes/logs/` for unauthorized access attempts
   304→10. **Keep updated** — run `hermes update` regularly for security patches
   305→
   306→### Securing API Keys
   307→
   308→```bash
   309→# Set proper permissions on the .env file
   310→chmod 600 ~/.hermes/.env
   311→
   312→# Keep separate keys for different services
   313→# Never commit .env files to version control
   314→```
   315→
   316→### Network Isolation
   317→
   318→For maximum security, run the gateway on a separate machine or VM:
   319→
   320→```yaml
   321→terminal:
   322→  backend: ssh
   323→  ssh_host: "agent-worker.local"
   324→  ssh_user: "hermes"
   325→  ssh_key: "~/.ssh/hermes_agent_key"
   326→```
   327→
   328→This keeps the gateway's messaging connections separate from the agent's command execution.
   329→
```

```
1→---
     2→sidebar_position: 2
     3→title: "Configuration"
     4→description: "Configure Hermes Agent — config.yaml, providers, models, API keys, and more"
     5→---
     6→
     7→# Configuration
     8→
     9→All settings are stored in the `~/.hermes/` directory for easy access.
    10→
    11→## Directory Structure
    12→
    13→```text
    14→~/.hermes/
    15→├── config.yaml     # Settings (model, terminal, TTS, compression, etc.)
    16→├── .env            # API keys and secrets
    17→├── auth.json       # OAuth provider credentials (Nous Portal, etc.)
    18→├── SOUL.md         # Optional: global persona (agent embodies this personality)
    19→├── memories/       # Persistent memory (MEMORY.md, USER.md)
    20→├── skills/         # Agent-created skills (managed via skill_manage tool)
    21→├── cron/           # Scheduled jobs
    22→├── sessions/       # Gateway sessions
    23→└── logs/           # Logs (errors.log, gateway.log — secrets auto-redacted)
    24→```
    25→
    26→## Managing Configuration
    27→
    28→```bash
    29→hermes config              # View current configuration
    30→hermes config edit         # Open config.yaml in your editor
    31→hermes config set KEY VAL  # Set a specific value
    32→hermes config check        # Check for missing options (after updates)
    33→hermes config migrate      # Interactively add missing options
    34→
    35→# Examples:
    36→hermes config set model anthropic/claude-opus-4
    37→hermes config set terminal.backend docker
    38→hermes config set OPENROUTER_API_KEY sk-or-...  # Saves to .env
    39→```
    40→
    41→:::tip
    42→The `hermes config set` command automatically routes values to the right file — API keys are saved to `.env`, everything else to `config.yaml`.
    43→:::
    44→
    45→## Configuration Precedence
    46→
    47→Settings are resolved in this order (highest priority first):
    48→
    49→1. **CLI arguments** — e.g., `hermes chat --model anthropic/claude-sonnet-4` (per-invocation override)
    50→2. **`~/.hermes/config.yaml`** — the primary config file for all non-secret settings
    51→3. **`~/.hermes/.env`** — fallback for env vars; **required** for secrets (API keys, tokens, passwords)
    52→4. **Built-in defaults** — hardcoded safe defaults when nothing else is set
    53→
    54→:::info Rule of Thumb
    55→Secrets (API keys, bot tokens, passwords) go in `.env`. Everything else (model, terminal backend, compression settings, memory limits, toolsets) goes in `config.yaml`. When both are set, `config.yaml` wins for non-secret settings.
    56→:::
    57→
    58→## Inference Providers
    59→
    60→You need at least one way to connect to an LLM. Use `hermes model` to switch providers and models interactively, or configure directly:
    61→
    62→| Provider | Setup |
    63→|----------|-------|
    64→| **Nous Portal** | `hermes model` (OAuth, subscription-based) |
    65→| **OpenAI Codex** | `hermes model` (ChatGPT OAuth, uses Codex models) |
    66→| **Anthropic** | `hermes model` (API key, setup-token, or Claude Code auto-detect) |
    67→| **OpenRouter** | `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` in `~/.hermes/.env` |
    68→| **z.ai / GLM** | `GLM_API_KEY` in `~/.hermes/.env` (provider: `zai`) |
    69→| **Kimi / Moonshot** | `KIMI_API_KEY` in `~/.hermes/.env` (provider: `kimi-coding`) |
    70→| **MiniMax** | `MINIMAX_API_KEY` in `~/.hermes/.env` (provider: `minimax`) |
    71→| **MiniMax China** | `MINIMAX_CN_API_KEY` in `~/.hermes/.env` (provider: `minimax-cn`) |
    72→| **Custom Endpoint** | `OPENAI_BASE_URL` + `OPENAI_API_KEY` in `~/.hermes/.env` |
    73→
    74→:::info Codex Note
    75→The OpenAI Codex provider authenticates via device code (open a URL, enter a code). Credentials are stored at `~/.codex/auth.json` and auto-refresh. No Codex CLI installation required.
    76→:::
    77→
    78→:::warning
    79→Even when using Nous Portal, Codex, or a custom endpoint, some tools (vision, web summarization, MoA) use a separate "auxiliary" model — by default Gemini Flash via OpenRouter. An `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` enables these tools automatically. You can also configure which model and provider these tools use — see [Auxiliary Models](#auxiliary-models) below.
    80→:::
    81→
    82→### Anthropic (Native)
    83→
    84→Use Claude models directly through the Anthropic API — no OpenRouter proxy needed. Supports three auth methods:
    85→
    86→```bash
    87→# With an API key (pay-per-token)
    88→export [REDACTED]
    89→hermes chat --provider anthropic --model claude-sonnet-4-6
    90→
    91→# With a Claude Code setup-token (Pro/Max subscription)
    92→export [REDACTED]  # from 'claude setup-token'
    93→hermes chat --provider anthropic
    94→
    95→# Auto-detect Claude Code credentials (if you have Claude Code installed)
    96→hermes chat --provider anthropic  # reads ~/.claude.json automatically
    97→```
    98→
    99→Or set it permanently:
   100→```yaml
   101→model:
   102→  provider: "anthropic"
   103→  default: "claude-sonnet-4-6"
   104→```
   105→
   106→:::tip Aliases
   107→`--provider claude` and `--provider claude-code` also work as shorthand for `--provider anthropic`.
   108→:::
   109→
   110→### First-Class Chinese AI Providers
   111→
   112→These providers have built-in support with dedicated provider IDs. Set the API key and use `--provider` to select:
   113→
   114→```bash
   115→# z.ai / ZhipuAI GLM
   116→hermes chat --provider zai --model glm-4-plus
   117→# Requires: GLM_API_KEY in ~/.hermes/.env
   118→
   119→# Kimi / Moonshot AI
   120→hermes chat --provider kimi-coding --model moonshot-v1-auto
   121→# Requires: KIMI_API_KEY in ~/.hermes/.env
   122→
   123→# MiniMax (global endpoint)
   124→hermes chat --provider minimax --model MiniMax-Text-01
   125→# Requires: MINIMAX_API_KEY in ~/.hermes/.env
   126→
   127→# MiniMax (China endpoint)
   128→hermes chat --provider minimax-cn --model MiniMax-Text-01
   129→# Requires: MINIMAX_CN_API_KEY in ~/.hermes/.env
   130→```
   131→
   132→Or set the provider permanently in `config.yaml`:
   133→```yaml
   134→model:
   135→  provider: "zai"       # or: kimi-coding, minimax, minimax-cn
   136→  default: "glm-4-plus"
   137→```
   138→
   139→Base URLs can be overridden with `GLM_BASE_URL`, `KIMI_BASE_URL`, `MINIMAX_BASE_URL`, or `MINIMAX_CN_BASE_URL` environment variables.
   140→
   141→## Custom & Self-Hosted LLM Providers
   142→
   143→Hermes Agent works with **any OpenAI-compatible API endpoint**. If a server implements `/v1/chat/completions`, you can point Hermes at it. This means you can use local models, GPU inference servers, multi-provider routers, or any third-party API.
   144→
   145→### General Setup
   146→
   147→Two ways to configure a custom endpoint:
   148→
   149→**Interactive (recommended):**
   150→```bash
   151→hermes model
   152→# Select "Custom endpoint (self-hosted / VLLM / etc.)"
   153→# Enter: API base URL, API key, Model name
   154→```
   155→
   156→**Manual (`.env` file):**
   157→```bash
   158→# Add to ~/.hermes/.env
   159→OPENAI_BASE_URL=http://localhost:8000/v1
   160→[REDACTED]
   161→LLM_MODEL=your-model-name
   162→```
   163→
   164→Everything below follows this same pattern — just change the URL, key, and model name.
   165→
   166→---
   167→
   168→### Ollama — Local Models, Zero Config
   169→
   170→[Ollama](https://ollama.com/) runs open-weight models locally with one command. Best for: quick local experimentation, privacy-sensitive work, offline use.
   171→
   172→```bash
   173→# Install and run a model
   174→ollama pull llama3.1:70b
   175→ollama serve   # Starts on port 11434
   176→
   177→# Configure Hermes
   178→OPENAI_BASE_URL=http://localhost:11434/v1
   179→OPENAI_API_KEY=ollama           # Any non-empty string
   180→LLM_MODEL=llama3.1:70b
   181→```
   182→
   183→Ollama's OpenAI-compatible endpoint supports chat completions, streaming, and tool calling (for supported models). No GPU required for smaller models — Ollama handles CPU inference automatically.
   184→
   185→:::tip
   186→List available models with `ollama list`. Pull any model from the [Ollama library](https://ollama.com/library) with `ollama pull <model>`.
   187→:::
   188→
   189→---
   190→
   191→### vLLM — High-Performance GPU Inference
   192→
   193→[vLLM](https://docs.vllm.ai/) is the standard for production LLM serving. Best for: maximum throughput on GPU hardware, serving large models, continuous batching.
   194→
   195→```bash
   196→# Start vLLM server
   197→pip install vllm
   198→vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct \
   199→  --port 8000 \
   200→  --tensor-parallel-size 2    # Multi-GPU
   201→
   202→# Configure Hermes
   203→OPENAI_BASE_URL=http://localhost:8000/v1
   204→OPENAI_API_KEY=dummy
   205→LLM_MODEL=meta-llama/Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct
   206→```
   207→
   208→vLLM supports tool calling, structured output, and multi-modal models. Use `--enable-auto-tool-choice` and `--tool-call-parser hermes` for Hermes-format tool calling with NousResearch models.
   209→
   210→---
   211→
   212→### SGLang — Fast Serving with RadixAttention
   213→
   214→[SGLang](https://github.com/sgl-project/sglang) is an alternative to vLLM with RadixAttention for KV cache reuse. Best for: multi-turn conversations (prefix caching), constrained decoding, structured output.
   215→
   216→```bash
   217→# Start SGLang server
   218→pip install sglang[all]
   219→python -m sglang.launch_server \
   220→  --model meta-llama/Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct \
   221→  --port 8000 \
   222→  --tp 2
   223→
   224→# Configure Hermes
   225→OPENAI_BASE_URL=http://localhost:8000/v1
   226→OPENAI_API_KEY=dummy
   227→LLM_MODEL=meta-llama/Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct
   228→```
   229→
   230→---
   231→
   232→### llama.cpp / llama-server — CPU & Metal Inference
   233→
   234→[llama.cpp](https://github.com/ggml-org/llama.cpp) runs quantized models on CPU, Apple Silicon (Metal), and consumer GPUs. Best for: running models without a datacenter GPU, Mac users, edge deployment.
   235→
   236→```bash
   237→# Build and start llama-server
   238→cmake -B build && cmake --build build --config Release
   239→./build/bin/llama-server \
   240→  -m models/llama-3.1-8b-instruct-Q4_K_M.gguf \
   241→  --port 8080 --host 0.0.0.0
   242→
   243→# Configure Hermes
   244→OPENAI_BASE_URL=http://localhost:8080/v1
   245→OPENAI_API_KEY=dummy
   246→LLM_MODEL=llama-3.1-8b-instruct
   247→```
   248→
   249→:::tip
   250→Download GGUF models from [Hugging Face](https://huggingface.co/models?library=gguf). Q4_K_M quantization offers the best balance of quality vs. memory usage.
   251→:::
   252→
   253→---
   254→
   255→### LiteLLM Proxy — Multi-Provider Gateway
   256→
   257→[LiteLLM](https://docs.litellm.ai/) is an OpenAI-compatible proxy that unifies 100+ LLM providers behind a single API. Best for: switching between providers without config changes, load balancing, fallback chains, budget controls.
   258→
   259→```bash
   260→# Install and start
   261→pip install litellm[proxy]
   262→litellm --model anthropic/claude-sonnet-4 --port 4000
   263→
   264→# Or with a config file for multiple models:
   265→litellm --config litellm_config.yaml --port 4000
   266→
   267→# Configure Hermes
   268→OPENAI_BASE_URL=http://localhost:4000/v1
   269→[REDACTED]
   270→LLM_MODEL=anthropic/claude-sonnet-4
   271→```
   272→
   273→Example `litellm_config.yaml` with fallback:
   274→```yaml
   275→model_list:
   276→  - model_name: "best"
   277→    litellm_params:
   278→      model: anthropic/claude-sonnet-4
   279→      api_key: sk-ant-...
   280→  - model_name: "best"
   281→    litellm_params:
   282→      model: openai/gpt-4o
   283→      api_key: sk-...
   284→router_settings:
   285→  routing_strategy: "latency-based-routing"
   286→```
   287→
   288→---
   289→
   290→### ClawRouter — Cost-Optimized Routing
   291→
   292→[ClawRouter](https://github.com/BlockRunAI/ClawRouter) by BlockRunAI is a local routing proxy that auto-selects models based on query complexity. It classifies requests across 14 dimensions and routes to the cheapest model that can handle the task. Payment is via USDC cryptocurrency (no API keys).
   293→
   294→```bash
   295→# Install and start
   296→npx @blockrun/clawrouter    # Starts on port 8402
   297→
   298→# Configure Hermes
   299→OPENAI_BASE_URL=http://localhost:8402/v1
   300→OPENAI_API_KEY=dummy
   301→LLM_MODEL=blockrun/auto     # or: blockrun/eco, blockrun/premium, blockrun/agentic
   302→```
   303→
   304→Routing profiles:
   305→| Profile | Strategy | Savings |
   306→|---------|----------|---------|
   307→| `blockrun/auto` | Balanced quality/cost | 74-100% |
   308→| `blockrun/eco` | Cheapest possible | 95-100% |
   309→| `blockrun/premium` | Best quality models | 0% |
   310→| `blockrun/free` | Free models only | 100% |
   311→| `blockrun/agentic` | Optimized for tool use | varies |
   312→
   313→:::note
   314→ClawRouter requires a USDC-funded wallet on Base or Solana for payment. All requests route through BlockRun's backend API. Run `npx @blockrun/clawrouter doctor` to check wallet status.
   315→:::
   316→
   317→---
   318→
   319→### Other Compatible Providers
   320→
   321→Any service with an OpenAI-compatible API works. Some popular options:
   322→
   323→| Provider | Base URL | Notes |
   324→|----------|----------|-------|
   325→| [Together AI](https://together.ai) | `https://api.together.xyz/v1` | Cloud-hosted open models |
   326→| [Groq](https://groq.com) | `https://api.groq.com/openai/v1` | Ultra-fast inference |
   327→| [DeepSeek](https://deepseek.com) | `https://api.deepseek.com/v1` | DeepSeek models |
   328→| [Fireworks AI](https://fireworks.ai) | `https://api.fireworks.ai/inference/v1` | Fast open model hosting |
   329→| [Cerebras](https://cerebras.ai) | `https://api.cerebras.ai/v1` | Wafer-scale chip inference |
   330→| [Mistral AI](https://mistral.ai) | `https://api.mistral.ai/v1` | Mistral models |
   331→| [OpenAI](https://openai.com) | `https://api.openai.com/v1` | Direct OpenAI access |
   332→| [Azure OpenAI](https://azure.microsoft.com) | `https://YOUR.openai.azure.com/` | Enterprise OpenAI |
   333→| [LocalAI](https://localai.io) | `http://localhost:8080/v1` | Self-hosted, multi-model |
   334→| [Jan](https://jan.ai) | `http://localhost:1337/v1` | Desktop app with local models |
   335→
   336→```bash
   337→# Example: Together AI
   338→OPENAI_BASE_URL=https://api.together.xyz/v1
   339→[REDACTED]
   340→LLM_MODEL=meta-llama/Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct-Turbo
   341→```
   342→
   343→---
   344→
   345→### Choosing the Right Setup
   346→
   347→| Use Case | Recommended |
   348→|----------|-------------|
   349→| **Just want it to work** | OpenRouter (default) or Nous Portal |
   350→| **Local models, easy setup** | Ollama |
   351→| **Production GPU serving** | vLLM or SGLang |
   352→| **Mac / no GPU** | Ollama or llama.cpp |
   353→| **Multi-provider routing** | LiteLLM Proxy or OpenRouter |
   354→| **Cost optimization** | ClawRouter or OpenRouter with `sort: "price"` |
   355→| **Maximum privacy** | Ollama, vLLM, or llama.cpp (fully local) |
   356→| **Enterprise / Azure** | Azure OpenAI with custom endpoint |
   357→| **Chinese AI models** | z.ai (GLM), Kimi/Moonshot, or MiniMax (first-class providers) |
   358→
   359→:::tip
   360→You can switch between providers at any time with `hermes model` — no restart required. Your conversation history, memory, and skills carry over regardless of which provider you use.
   361→:::
   362→
   363→## Optional API Keys
   364→
   365→| Feature | Provider | Env Variable |
   366→|---------|----------|--------------|
   367→| Web scraping | [Firecrawl](https://firecrawl.dev/) | `FIRECRAWL_API_KEY` |
   368→| Browser automation | [Browserbase](https://browserbase.com/) | `BROWSERBASE_API_KEY`, `BROWSERBASE_PROJECT_ID` |
   369→| Image generation | [FAL](https://fal.ai/) | `FAL_KEY` |
   370→| Premium TTS voices | [ElevenLabs](https://elevenlabs.io/) | `ELEVENLABS_API_KEY` |
   371→| OpenAI TTS + voice transcription | [OpenAI](https://platform.openai.com/api-keys) | `VOICE_TOOLS_OPENAI_KEY` |
   372→| RL Training | [Tinker](https://tinker-console.thinkingmachines.ai/) + [WandB](https://wandb.ai/) | `TINKER_API_KEY`, `WANDB_API_KEY` |
   373→| Cross-session user modeling | [Honcho](https://honcho.dev/) | `HONCHO_API_KEY` |
   374→
   375→### Self-Hosting Firecrawl
   376→
   377→By default, Hermes uses the [Firecrawl cloud API](https://firecrawl.dev/) for web search and scraping. If you prefer to run Firecrawl locally, you can point Hermes at a self-hosted instance instead.
   378→
   379→**What you get:** No API key required, no rate limits, no per-page costs, full data sovereignty.
   380→
   381→**What you lose:** The cloud version uses Firecrawl's proprietary "Fire-engine" for advanced anti-bot bypassing (Cloudflare, CAPTCHAs, IP rotation). Self-hosted uses basic fetch + Playwright, so some protected sites may fail. Search uses DuckDuckGo instead of Google.
   382→
   383→**Setup:**
   384→
   385→1. Clone and start the Firecrawl Docker stack (5 containers: API, Playwright, Redis, RabbitMQ, PostgreSQL — requires ~4-8 GB RAM):
   386→   ```bash
   387→   git clone https://github.com/mendableai/firecrawl
   388→   cd firecrawl
   389→   # In .env, set: USE_DB_AUTHENTICATION=false
   390→   docker compose up -d
   391→   ```
   392→
   393→2. Point Hermes at your instance (no API key needed):
   394→   ```bash
   395→   hermes config set FIRECRAWL_API_URL http://localhost:3002
   396→   ```
   397→
   398→You can also set both `FIRECRAWL_API_KEY` and `FIRECRAWL_API_URL` if your self-hosted instance has authentication enabled.
   399→
   400→## OpenRouter Provider Routing
   401→
   402→When using OpenRouter, you can control how requests are routed across providers. Add a `provider_routing` section to `~/.hermes/config.yaml`:
   403→
   404→```yaml
   405→provider_routing:
   406→  sort: "throughput"          # "price" (default), "throughput", or "latency"
   407→  # only: ["anthropic"]      # Only use these providers
   408→  # ignore: ["deepinfra"]    # Skip these providers
   409→  # order: ["anthropic", "google"]  # Try providers in this order
   410→  # require_parameters: true  # Only use providers that support all request params
   411→  # data_collection: "deny"   # Exclude providers that may store/train on data
   412→```
   413→
   414→**Shortcuts:** Append `:nitro` to any model name for throughput sorting (e.g., `anthropic/claude-sonnet-4:nitro`), or `:floor` for price sorting.
   415→
   416→## Terminal Backend Configuration
   417→
   418→Configure which environment the agent uses for terminal commands:
   419→
   420→```yaml
   421→terminal:
   422→  backend: local    # or: docker, ssh, singularity, modal, daytona
   423→  cwd: "."          # Working directory ("." = current dir)
   424→  timeout: 180      # Command timeout in seconds
   425→
   426→  # Docker-specific settings
   427→  docker_image: "nikolaik/python-nodejs:python3.11-nodejs20"
   428→  docker_volumes:                    # Share host directories with the container
   429→    - "/home/user/projects:/workspace/projects"
   430→    - "/home/user/data:/data:ro"     # :ro for read-only
   431→
   432→  # Container resource limits (docker, singularity, modal, daytona)
   433→  container_cpu: 1                   # CPU cores
   434→  container_memory: 5120             # MB (default 5GB)
   435→  container_disk: 51200              # MB (default 50GB)
   436→  container_persistent: true         # Persist filesystem across sessions
   437→```
   438→
   439→### Docker Volume Mounts
   440→
   441→When using the Docker backend, `docker_volumes` lets you share host directories with the container. Each entry uses standard Docker `-v` syntax: `host_path:container_path[:options]`.
   442→
   443→```yaml
   444→terminal:
   445→  backend: docker
   446→  docker_volumes:
   447→    - "/home/user/projects:/workspace/projects"   # Read-write (default)
   448→    - "/home/user/datasets:/data:ro"              # Read-only
   449→    - "/home/user/outputs:/outputs"               # Agent writes, you read
   450→```
   451→
   452→This is useful for:
   453→- **Providing files** to the agent (datasets, configs, reference code)
   454→- **Receiving files** from the agent (generated code, reports, exports)
   455→- **Shared workspaces** where both you and the agent access the same files
   456→
   457→Can also be set via environment variable: `TERMINAL_DOCKER_VOLUMES='["/host:/container"]'` (JSON array).
   458→
   459→See [Code Execution](features/code-execution.md) and the [Terminal section of the README](features/tools.md) for details on each backend.
   460→
   461→## Memory Configuration
   462→
   463→```yaml
   464→memory:
   465→  memory_enabled: true
   466→  user_profile_enabled: true
   467→  memory_char_limit: 2200   # ~800 tokens
   468→  user_char_limit: 1375     # ~500 tokens
   469→```
   470→
   471→## Git Worktree Isolation
   472→
   473→Enable isolated git worktrees for running multiple agents in parallel on the same repo:
   474→
   475→```yaml
   476→worktree: true    # Always create a worktree (same as hermes -w)
   477→# worktree: false # Default — only when -w flag is passed
   478→```
   479→
   480→When enabled, each CLI session creates a fresh worktree under `.worktrees/` with its own branch. Agents can edit files, commit, push, and create PRs without interfering with each other. Clean worktrees are removed on exit; dirty ones are kept for manual recovery.
   481→
   482→You can also list gitignored files to copy into worktrees via `.worktreeinclude` in your repo root:
   483→
   484→```
   485→# .worktreeinclude
   486→.env
   487→.venv/
   488→node_modules/
   489→```
   490→
   491→## Context Compression
   492→
   493→```yaml
   494→compression:
   495→  enabled: true
   496→  threshold: 0.85              # Compress at 85% of context limit
   497→  summary_model: "google/gemini-3-flash-preview"   # Model for summarization
   498→  # summary_provider: "auto"   # "auto", "openrouter", "nous", "main"
   499→```
   500→
   501→The `summary_model` must support a context length at least as large as your main model's, since it receives the full middle section of the conversation for compression.
   502→
   503→## Iteration Budget Pressure
   504→
   505→When the agent is working on a complex task with many tool calls, it can burn through its iteration budget (default: 90 turns) without realizing it's running low. Budget pressure automatically warns the model as it approaches the limit:
   506→
   507→| Threshold | Level | What the model sees |
   508→|-----------|-------|---------------------|
   509→| **70%** | Caution | `[BUDGET: 63/90. 27 iterations left. Start consolidating.]` |
   510→| **90%** | Warning | `[BUDGET WARNING: 81/90. Only 9 left. Respond NOW.]` |
   511→
   512→Warnings are injected into the last tool result's JSON (as a `_budget_warning` field) rather than as separate messages — this preserves prompt caching and doesn't disrupt the conversation structure.
   513→
   514→```yaml
   515→agent:
   516→  max_turns: 90                # Max iterations per conversation turn (default: 90)
   517→```
   518→
   519→Budget pressure is enabled by default. The agent sees warnings naturally as part of tool results, encouraging it to consolidate its work and deliver a response before running out of iterations.
   520→
   521→## Auxiliary Models
   522→
   523→Hermes uses lightweight "auxiliary" models for side tasks like image analysis, web page summarization, and browser screenshot analysis. By default, these use **Gemini Flash** via OpenRouter or Nous Portal — you don't need to configure anything.
   524→
   525→To use a different model, add an `auxiliary` section to `~/.hermes/config.yaml`:
   526→
   527→```yaml
   528→auxiliary:
   529→  # Image analysis (vision_analyze tool + browser screenshots)
   530→  vision:
   531→    provider: "auto"           # "auto", "openrouter", "nous", "main"
   532→    model: ""                  # e.g. "openai/gpt-4o", "google/gemini-2.5-flash"
   533→
   534→  # Web page summarization + browser page text extraction
   535→  web_extract:
   536→    provider: "auto"
   537→    model: ""                  # e.g. "google/gemini-2.5-flash"
   538→```
   539→
   540→### Changing the Vision Model
   541→
   542→To use GPT-4o instead of Gemini Flash for image analysis:
   543→
   544→```yaml
   545→auxiliary:
   546→  vision:
   547→    model: "openai/gpt-4o"
   548→```
   549→
   550→Or via environment variable (in `~/.hermes/.env`):
   551→
   552→```bash
   553→AUXILIARY_VISION_MODEL=openai/gpt-4o
   554→```
   555→
   556→### Provider Options
   557→
   558→| Provider | Description | Requirements |
   559→|----------|-------------|-------------|
   560→| `"auto"` | Best available (default). Vision tries OpenRouter → Nous → Codex. | — |
   561→| `"openrouter"` | Force OpenRouter — routes to any model (Gemini, GPT-4o, Claude, etc.) | `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` |
   562→| `"nous"` | Force Nous Portal | `hermes login` |
   563→| `"codex"` | Force Codex OAuth (ChatGPT account). Supports vision (gpt-5.3-codex). | `hermes model` → Codex |
   564→| `"main"` | Use your custom endpoint (`OPENAI_BASE_URL` + `OPENAI_API_KEY`). Works with OpenAI, local models, or any OpenAI-compatible API. | `OPENAI_BASE_URL` + `OPENAI_API_KEY` |
   565→
   566→### Common Setups
   567→
   568→**Using OpenAI API key for vision:**
   569→```yaml
   570→# In ~/.hermes/.env:
   571→# OPENAI_BASE_URL=https://api.openai.com/v1
   572→# OPENAI_API_KEY=sk-...
   573→
   574→auxiliary:
   575→  vision:
   576→    provider: "main"
   577→    model: "gpt-4o"       # or "gpt-4o-mini" for cheaper
   578→```
   579→
   580→**Using OpenRouter for vision** (route to any model):
   581→```yaml
   582→auxiliary:
   583→  vision:
   584→    provider: "openrouter"
   585→    model: "openai/gpt-4o"      # or "google/gemini-2.5-flash", etc.
   586→```
   587→
   588→**Using Codex OAuth** (ChatGPT Pro/Plus account — no API key needed):
   589→```yaml
   590→auxiliary:
   591→  vision:
   592→    provider: "codex"     # uses your ChatGPT OAuth token
   593→    # model defaults to gpt-5.3-codex (supports vision)
   594→```
   595→
   596→**Using a local/self-hosted model:**
   597→```yaml
   598→auxiliary:
   599→  vision:
   600→    provider: "main"      # uses your OPENAI_BASE_URL endpoint
   601→    model: "my-local-model"
   602→```
   603→
   604→:::tip
   605→If you use Codex OAuth as your main model provider, vision works automatically — no extra configuration needed. Codex is included in the auto-detection chain for vision.
   606→:::
   607→
   608→:::warning
   609→**Vision requires a multimodal model.** If you set `provider: "main"`, make sure your endpoint supports multimodal/vision — otherwise image analysis will fail.
   610→:::
   611→
   612→### Environment Variables
   613→
   614→You can also configure auxiliary models via environment variables instead of `config.yaml`:
   615→
   616→| Setting | Environment Variable |
   617→|---------|---------------------|
   618→| Vision provider | `AUXILIARY_VISION_PROVIDER` |
   619→| Vision model | `AUXILIARY_VISION_MODEL` |
   620→| Web extract provider | `AUXILIARY_WEB_EXTRACT_PROVIDER` |
   621→| Web extract model | `AUXILIARY_WEB_EXTRACT_MODEL` |
   622→| Compression provider | `CONTEXT_COMPRESSION_PROVIDER` |
   623→| Compression model | `CONTEXT_COMPRESSION_MODEL` |
   624→
   625→:::tip
   626→Run `hermes config` to see your current auxiliary model settings. Overrides only show up when they differ from the defaults.
   627→:::
   628→
   629→## Reasoning Effort
   630→
   631→Control how much "thinking" the model does before responding:
   632→
   633→```yaml
   634→agent:
   635→  reasoning_effort: ""   # empty = medium (default). Options: xhigh (max), high, medium, low, minimal, none
   636→```
   637→
   638→When unset (default), reasoning effort defaults to "medium" — a balanced level that works well for most tasks. Setting a value overrides it — higher reasoning effort gives better results on complex tasks at the cost of more tokens and latency.
   639→
   640→You can also change the reasoning effort at runtime with the `/reasoning` command:
   641→
   642→```
   643→/reasoning           # Show current effort level and display state
   644→/reasoning high      # Set reasoning effort to high
   645→/reasoning none      # Disable reasoning
   646→/reasoning show      # Show model thinking above each response
   647→/reasoning hide      # Hide model thinking
   648→```
   649→
   650→## TTS Configuration
   651→
   652→```yaml
   653→tts:
   654→  provider: "edge"              # "edge" | "elevenlabs" | "openai"
   655→  edge:
   656→    voice: "en-US-AriaNeural"   # 322 voices, 74 languages
   657→  elevenlabs:
   658→    voice_id: "pNInz6obpgDQGcFmaJgB"
   659→    model_id: "eleven_multilingual_v2"
   660→  openai:
   661→    model: "gpt-4o-mini-tts"
   662→    voice: "alloy"              # alloy, echo, fable, onyx, nova, shimmer
   663→```
   664→
   665→## Display Settings
   666→
   667→```yaml
   668→display:
   669→  tool_progress: all    # off | new | all | verbose
   670→  personality: "kawaii"  # Default personality for the CLI
   671→  compact: false         # Compact output mode (less whitespace)
   672→  resume_display: full   # full (show previous messages on resume) | minimal (one-liner only)
   673→  bell_on_complete: false  # Play terminal bell when agent finishes (great for long tasks)
   674→  show_reasoning: false    # Show model reasoning/thinking above each response (toggle with /reasoning show|hide)
   675→```
   676→
   677→| Mode | What you see |
   678→|------|-------------|
   679→| `off` | Silent — just the final response |
   680→| `new` | Tool indicator only when the tool changes |
   681→| `all` | Every tool call with a short preview (default) |
   682→| `verbose` | Full args, results, and debug logs |
   683→
   684→## Speech-to-Text (STT)
   685→
   686→```yaml
   687→stt:
   688→  provider: "openai"           # STT provider
   689→```
   690→
   691→Requires `VOICE_TOOLS_OPENAI_KEY` in `.env` for OpenAI STT.
   692→
   693→## Quick Commands
   694→
   695→Define custom commands that run shell commands without invoking the LLM — zero token usage, instant execution. Especially useful from messaging platforms (Telegram, Discord, etc.) for quick server checks or utility scripts.
   696→
   697→```yaml
   698→quick_commands:
   699→  status:
   700→    type: exec
   701→    command: systemctl status hermes-agent
   702→  disk:
   703→    type: exec
   704→    command: df -h /
   705→  update:
   706→    type: exec
   707→    command: cd ~/.hermes/hermes-agent && git pull && pip install -e .
   708→  gpu:
   709→    type: exec
   710→    command: nvidia-smi --query-gpu=name,utilization.gpu,memory.used,memory.total --format=csv,noheader
   711→```
   712→
   713→Usage: type `/status`, `/disk`, `/update`, or `/gpu` in the CLI or any messaging platform. The command runs locally on the host and returns the output directly — no LLM call, no tokens consumed.
   714→
   715→- **30-second timeout** — long-running commands are killed with an error message
   716→- **Priority** — quick commands are checked before skill commands, so you can override skill names
   717→- **Type** — only `exec` is supported (runs a shell command); other types show an error
   718→- **Works everywhere** — CLI, Telegram, Discord, Slack, WhatsApp, Signal
   719→
   720→## Human Delay
   721→
   722→Simulate human-like response pacing in messaging platforms:
   723→
   724→```yaml
   725→human_delay:
   726→  mode: "off"                  # off | natural | custom
   727→  min_ms: 500                  # Minimum delay (custom mode)
   728→  max_ms: 2000                 # Maximum delay (custom mode)
   729→```
   730→
   731→## Code Execution
   732→
   733→Configure the sandboxed Python code execution tool:
   734→
   735→```yaml
   736→code_execution:
   737→  timeout: 300                 # Max execution time in seconds
   738→  max_tool_calls: 50           # Max tool calls within code execution
   739→```
   740→
   741→## Browser
   742→
   743→Configure browser automation behavior:
   744→
   745→```yaml
   746→browser:
   747→  inactivity_timeout: 120        # Seconds before auto-closing idle sessions
   748→  record_sessions: false         # Auto-record browser sessions as WebM videos to ~/.hermes/browser_recordings/
   749→```
   750→
   751→## Checkpoints
   752→
   753→Automatic filesystem snapshots before destructive file operations. See the [Checkpoints feature page](/docs/user-guide/features/checkpoints) for details.
   754→
   755→```yaml
   756→checkpoints:
   757→  enabled: false                 # Enable automatic checkpoints (also: hermes --checkpoints)
   758→  max_snapshots: 50              # Max checkpoints to keep per directory
   759→```
   760→
   761→
   762→## Delegation
   763→
   764→Configure subagent behavior for the delegate tool:
   765→
   766→```yaml
   767→delegation:
   768→  max_iterations: 50           # Max iterations per subagent
   769→  default_toolsets:             # Toolsets available to subagents
   770→    - terminal
   771→    - file
   772→    - web
   773→  # model: "google/gemini-3-flash-preview"  # Override model (empty = inherit parent)
   774→  # provider: "openrouter"                  # Override provider (empty = inherit parent)
   775→```
   776→
   777→**Subagent provider:model override:** By default, subagents inherit the parent agent's provider and model. Set `delegation.provider` and `delegation.model` to route subagents to a different provider:model pair — e.g., use a cheap/fast model for narrowly-scoped subtasks while your primary agent runs an expensive reasoning model.
   778→
   779→The delegation provider uses the same credential resolution as CLI/gateway startup. All configured providers are supported: `openrouter`, `nous`, `zai`, `kimi-coding`, `minimax`, `minimax-cn`. When a provider is set, the system automatically resolves the correct base URL, API key, and API mode — no manual credential wiring needed.
   780→
   781→**Precedence:** `delegation.provider` in config → parent provider (inherited). `delegation.model` in config → parent model (inherited). Setting just `model` without `provider` changes only the model name while keeping the parent's credentials (useful for switching models within the same provider like OpenRouter).
   782→
   783→## Clarify
   784→
   785→Configure the clarification prompt behavior:
   786→
   787→```yaml
   788→clarify:
   789→  timeout: 120                 # Seconds to wait for user clarification response
   790→```
   791→
   792→## Context Files (SOUL.md, AGENTS.md)
   793→
   794→Drop these files in your project directory and the agent automatically picks them up:
   795→
   796→| File | Purpose |
   797→|------|---------|
   798→| `AGENTS.md` | Project-specific instructions, coding conventions |
   799→| `SOUL.md` | Persona definition — the agent embodies this personality |
   800→| `.cursorrules` | Cursor IDE rules (also detected) |
   801→| `.cursor/rules/*.mdc` | Cursor rule files (also detected) |
   802→
   803→- **AGENTS.md** is hierarchical: if subdirectories also have AGENTS.md, all are combined.
   804→- **SOUL.md** checks cwd first, then `~/.hermes/SOUL.md` as a global fallback.
   805→- All context files are capped at 20,000 characters with smart truncation.
   806→
   807→## Working Directory
   808→
   809→| Context | Default |
   810→|---------|---------|
   811→| **CLI (`hermes`)** | Current directory where you run the command |
   812→| **Messaging gateway** | Home directory `~` (override with `MESSAGING_CWD`) |
   813→| **Docker / Singularity / Modal / SSH** | User's home directory inside the container or remote machine |
   814→
   815→Override the working directory:
   816→```bash
   817→# In ~/.hermes/.env or ~/.hermes/config.yaml:
   818→MESSAGING_CWD=/home/myuser/projects    # Gateway sessions
   819→TERMINAL_CWD=/workspace                # All terminal sessions
   820→```
   821→
```

```
1→"""Dangerous command approval -- detection, prompting, and per-session state.
     2→
     3→This module is the single source of truth for the dangerous command system:
     4→- Pattern detection (DANGEROUS_PATTERNS, detect_dangerous_command)
     5→- Per-session approval state (thread-safe, keyed by session_key)
     6→- Approval prompting (CLI interactive + gateway async)
     7→- Permanent allowlist persistence (config.yaml)
     8→"""
     9→
    10→import logging
    11→import os
    12→import re
    13→import sys
    14→import threading
    15→from typing import Optional
    16→
    17→logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)
    18→
    19→# =========================================================================
    20→# Dangerous command patterns
    21→# =========================================================================
    22→
    23→DANGEROUS_PATTERNS = [
    24→    (r'\brm\s+(-[^\s]*\s+)*/', "delete in root path"),
    25→    (r'\brm\s+-[^\s]*r', "recursive delete"),
    26→    (r'\brm\s+--recursive\b', "recursive delete (long flag)"),
    27→    (r'\bchmod\s+(-[^\s]*\s+)*777\b', "world-writable permissions"),
    28→    (r'\bchmod\s+--recursive\b.*777', "recursive world-writable (long flag)"),
    29→    (r'\bchown\s+(-[^\s]*)?R\s+root', "recursive chown to root"),
    30→    (r'\bchown\s+--recursive\b.*root', "recursive chown to root (long flag)"),
    31→    (r'\bmkfs\b', "format filesystem"),
    32→    (r'\bdd\s+.*if=', "disk copy"),
    33→    (r'>\s*/dev/sd', "write to block device"),
    34→    (r'\bDROP\s+(TABLE|DATABASE)\b', "SQL DROP"),
    35→    (r'\bDELETE\s+FROM\b(?!.*\bWHERE\b)', "SQL DELETE without WHERE"),
    36→    (r'\bTRUNCATE\s+(TABLE)?\s*\w', "SQL TRUNCATE"),
    37→    (r'>\s*/etc/', "overwrite system config"),
    38→    (r'\bsystemctl\s+(stop|disable|mask)\b', "stop/disable system service"),
    39→    (r'\bkill\s+-9\s+-1\b', "kill all processes"),
    40→    (r'\bpkill\s+-9\b', "force kill processes"),
    41→    (r':()\s*{\s*:\s*\|\s*:&\s*}\s*;:', "fork bomb"),
    42→    (r'\b(bash|sh|zsh)\s+-c\s+', "shell command via -c flag"),
    43→    (r'\b(python[23]?|perl|ruby|node)\s+-[ec]\s+', "script execution via -e/-c flag"),
    44→    (r'\b(curl|wget)\b.*\|\s*(ba)?sh\b', "pipe remote content to shell"),
    45→    (r'\b(bash|sh|zsh|ksh)\s+<\s*<?\s*\(\s*(curl|wget)\b', "execute remote script via process substitution"),
    46→    (r'\btee\b.*(/etc/|/dev/sd|\.ssh/|\.hermes/\.env)', "overwrite system file via tee"),
    47→    (r'\bxargs\s+.*\brm\b', "xargs with rm"),
    48→    (r'\bfind\b.*-exec\s+(/\S*/)?rm\b', "find -exec rm"),
    49→    (r'\bfind\b.*-delete\b', "find -delete"),
    50→]
    51→
    52→
    53→# =========================================================================
    54→# Detection
    55→# =========================================================================
    56→
    57→def detect_dangerous_command(command: str) -> tuple:
    58→    """Check if a command matches any dangerous patterns.
    59→
    60→    Returns:
    61→        (is_dangerous, pattern_key, description) or (False, None, None)
    62→    """
    63→    command_lower = command.lower()
    64→    for pattern, description in DANGEROUS_PATTERNS:
    65→        if re.search(pattern, command_lower, re.IGNORECASE | re.DOTALL):
    66→            pattern_key = pattern.split(r'\b')[1] if r'\b' in pattern else pattern[:20]
    67→            return (True, pattern_key, description)
    68→    return (False, None, None)
    69→
    70→
    71→# =========================================================================
    72→# Per-session approval state (thread-safe)
    73→# =========================================================================
    74→
    75→_lock = threading.Lock()
    76→_pending: dict[str, dict] = {}
    77→_session_approved: dict[str, set] = {}
    78→_permanent_approved: set = set()
    79→
    80→
    81→def submit_pending(session_key: str, approval: dict):
    82→    """Store a pending approval request for a session."""
    83→    with _lock:
    84→        _pending[session_key] = approval
    85→
    86→
    87→def pop_pending(session_key: str) -> Optional[dict]:
    88→    """Retrieve and remove a pending approval for a session."""
    89→    with _lock:
    90→        return _pending.pop(session_key, None)
    91→
    92→
    93→def has_pending(session_key: str) -> bool:
    94→    """Check if a session has a pending approval request."""
    95→    with _lock:
    96→        return session_key in _pending
    97→
    98→
    99→def approve_session(session_key: str, pattern_key: str):
   100→    """Approve a pattern for this session only."""
   101→    with _lock:
   102→        _session_approved.setdefault(session_key, set()).add(pattern_key)
   103→
   104→
   105→def is_approved(session_key: str, pattern_key: str) -> bool:
   106→    """Check if a pattern is approved (session-scoped or permanent)."""
   107→    with _lock:
   108→        if pattern_key in _permanent_approved:
   109→            return True
   110→        return pattern_key in _session_approved.get(session_key, set())
   111→
   112→
   113→def approve_permanent(pattern_key: str):
   114→    """Add a pattern to the permanent allowlist."""
   115→    with _lock:
   116→        _permanent_approved.add(pattern_key)
   117→
   118→
   119→def load_permanent(patterns: set):
   120→    """Bulk-load permanent allowlist entries from config."""
   121→    with _lock:
   122→        _permanent_approved.update(patterns)
   123→
   124→
   125→def clear_session(session_key: str):
   126→    """Clear all approvals and pending requests for a session."""
   127→    with _lock:
   128→        _session_approved.pop(session_key, None)
   129→        _pending.pop(session_key, None)
   130→
   131→
   132→# =========================================================================
   133→# Config persistence for permanent allowlist
   134→# =========================================================================
   135→
   136→def load_permanent_allowlist() -> set:
   137→    """Load permanently allowed command patterns from config.
   138→
   139→    Also syncs them into the approval module so is_approved() works for
   140→    patterns added via 'always' in a previous session.
   141→    """
   142→    try:
   143→        from hermes_cli.config import load_config
   144→        config = load_config()
   145→        patterns = set(config.get("command_allowlist", []) or [])
   146→        if patterns:
   147→            load_permanent(patterns)
   148→        return patterns
   149→    except Exception:
   150→        return set()
   151→
   152→
   153→def save_permanent_allowlist(patterns: set):
   154→    """Save permanently allowed command patterns to config."""
   155→    try:
   156→        from hermes_cli.config import load_config, save_config
   157→        config = load_config()
   158→        config["command_allowlist"] = list(patterns)
   159→        save_config(config)
   160→    except Exception as e:
   161→        logger.warning("Could not save allowlist: %s", e)
   162→
   163→
   164→# =========================================================================
   165→# Approval prompting + orchestration
   166→# =========================================================================
   167→
   168→def prompt_dangerous_approval(command: str, description: str,
   169→                              timeout_seconds: int = 60,
   170→                              approval_callback=None) -> str:
   171→    """Prompt the user to approve a dangerous command (CLI only).
   172→
   173→    Args:
   174→        approval_callback: Optional callback registered by the CLI for
   175→            prompt_toolkit integration. Signature: (command, description) -> str.
   176→
   177→    Returns: 'once', 'session', 'always', or 'deny'
   178→    """
   179→    if approval_callback is not None:
   180→        try:
   181→            return approval_callback(command, description)
   182→        except Exception:
   183→            return "deny"
   184→
   185→    os.environ["HERMES_SPINNER_PAUSE"] = "1"
   186→    try:
   187→        is_truncated = len(command) > 80
   188→        while True:
   189→            print()
   190→            print(f"  ⚠️  DANGEROUS COMMAND: {description}")
   191→            print(f"      {command[:80]}{'...' if is_truncated else ''}")
   192→            print()
   193→            view_hint = "  |  [v]iew full" if is_truncated else ""
   194→            print(f"      [o]nce  |  [s]ession  |  [a]lways  |  [d]eny{view_hint}")
   195→            print()
   196→            sys.stdout.flush()
   197→
   198→            result = {"choice": ""}
   199→
   200→            def get_input():
   201→                try:
   202→                    result["choice"] = input("      Choice [o/s/a/D]: ").strip().lower()
   203→                except (EOFError, OSError):
   204→                    result["choice"] = ""
   205→
   206→            thread = threading.Thread(target=get_input, daemon=True)
   207→            thread.start()
   208→            thread.join(timeout=timeout_seconds)
   209→
   210→            if thread.is_alive():
   211→                print("\n      ⏱ Timeout - denying command")
   212→                return "deny"
   213→
   214→            choice = result["choice"]
   215→            if choice in ('v', 'view') and is_truncated:
   216→                print()
   217→                print("      Full command:")
   218→                print(f"      {command}")
   219→                is_truncated = False  # show full on next loop iteration too
   220→                continue
   221→            if choice in ('o', 'once'):
   222→                print("      ✓ Allowed once")
   223→                return "once"
   224→            elif choice in ('s', 'session'):
   225→                print("      ✓ Allowed for this session")
   226→                return "session"
   227→            elif choice in ('a', 'always'):
   228→                print("      ✓ Added to permanent allowlist")
   229→                return "always"
   230→            else:
   231→                print("      ✗ Denied")
   232→                return "deny"
   233→
   234→    except (EOFError, KeyboardInterrupt):
   235→        print("\n      ✗ Cancelled")
   236→        return "deny"
   237→    finally:
   238→        if "HERMES_SPINNER_PAUSE" in os.environ:
   239→            del os.environ["HERMES_SPINNER_PAUSE"]
   240→        print()
   241→        sys.stdout.flush()
   242→
   243→
   244→def check_dangerous_command(command: str, env_type: str,
   245→                            approval_callback=None) -> dict:
   246→    """Check if a command is dangerous and handle approval.
   247→
   248→    This is the main entry point called by terminal_tool before executing
   249→    any command. It orchestrates detection, session checks, and prompting.
   250→
   251→    Args:
   252→        command: The shell command to check.
   253→        env_type: Terminal backend type ('local', 'ssh', 'docker', etc.).
   254→        approval_callback: Optional CLI callback for interactive prompts.
   255→
   256→    Returns:
   257→        {"approved": True/False, "message": str or None, ...}
   258→    """
   259→    if env_type in ("docker", "singularity", "modal", "daytona"):
   260→        return {"approved": True, "message": None}
   261→
   262→    # --yolo: bypass all approval prompts
   263→    if os.getenv("HERMES_YOLO_MODE"):
   264→        return {"approved": True, "message": None}
   265→
   266→    is_dangerous, pattern_key, description = detect_dangerous_command(command)
   267→    if not is_dangerous:
   268→        return {"approved": True, "message": None}
   269→
   270→    session_key = os.getenv("HERMES_SESSION_KEY", "default")
   271→    if is_approved(session_key, pattern_key):
   272→        return {"approved": True, "message": None}
   273→
   274→    is_cli = os.getenv("HERMES_INTERACTIVE")
   275→    is_gateway = os.getenv("HERMES_GATEWAY_SESSION")
   276→
   277→    if not is_cli and not is_gateway:
   278→        return {"approved": True, "message": None}
   279→
   280→    if is_gateway or os.getenv("HERMES_EXEC_ASK"):
   281→        submit_pending(session_key, {
   282→            "command": command,
   283→            "pattern_key": pattern_key,
   284→            "description": description,
   285→        })
   286→        return {
   287→            "approved": False,
   288→            "pattern_key": pattern_key,
   289→            "status": "approval_required",
   290→            "command": command,
   291→            "description": description,
   292→            "message": f"⚠️ This command is potentially dangerous ({description}). Asking the user for approval...",
   293→        }
   294→
   295→    choice = prompt_dangerous_approval(command, description,
   296→                                       approval_callback=approval_callback)
   297→
   298→    if choice == "deny":
   299→        return {
   300→            "approved": False,
   301→            "message": f"BLOCKED: User denied this potentially dangerous command (matched '{description}' pattern). Do NOT retry this command - the user has explicitly rejected it.",
   302→            "pattern_key": pattern_key,
   303→            "description": description,
   304→        }
   305→
   306→    if choice == "session":
   307→        approve_session(session_key, pattern_key)
   308→    elif choice == "always":
   309→        approve_session(session_key, pattern_key)
   310→        approve_permanent(pattern_key)
   311→        save_permanent_allowlist(_permanent_approved)
   312→
   313→    return {"approved": True, "message": None}
   314→
```

    78→def _has_any_provider_configured() -> bool:

    81→    from hermes_cli.auth import get_auth_status

    93→    if any(os.getenv(v) for v in provider_env_vars):
    94→        return True
    95→
    96→    # Check .env file for keys
    97→    env_file = get_env_path()
    98→    if env_file.exists():
    99→        try:
   100→            for line in env_file.read_text().splitlines():
   101→                line = line.strip()
   102→                if line.startswith("#") or "=" not in line:
   103→                    continue
   104→                key, _, val = line.partition("=")
   105→                val = val.strip().strip("'\"")
   106→                if key.strip() in provider_env_vars and val:
   107→                    return True
   108→        except Exception:

   128→def _session_browse_picker(sessions: list) -> Optional[str]:
   129→    """Interactive curses-based session browser with live search filtering.
   130→
   131→    Returns the selected session ID, or None if cancelled.
   132→    Uses curses (not simple_term_menu) to avoid the ghost-duplication rendering
   133→    bug in tmux/iTerm when arrow keys are used.
   134→    """
   135→    if not sessions:
   136→        print("No sessions found.")
   137→        return None
   138→
   139→    # Try curses-based picker first
   140→    try:
   141→        import curses
   142→        import time as _time
   143→        from datetime import datetime
   144→
   145→        result_holder = [None]
   146→
   147→        def _relative_time(ts):
   148→            if not ts:
   149→                return "?"
   150→            delta = _time.time() - ts
   151→            if delta < 60:
   152→                return "just now"
   153→            elif delta < 3600:
   154→                return f"{int(delta / 60)}m ago"
   155→            elif delta < 86400:
   156→                return f"{int(delta / 3600)}h ago"
   157→            elif delta < 172800:
   158→                return "yesterday"
   159→            elif delta < 604800:
   160→                return f"{int(delta / 86400)}d ago"
   161→            else:
   162→                return datetime.fromtimestamp(ts).strftime("%Y-%m-%d")
   163→
   164→        def _format_row(s, max_x):
   165→            """Format a session row for display."""
   166→            title = (s.get("title") or "").strip()
   167→            preview = (s.get("preview") or "").strip()
   168→            source = s.get("source", "")[:6]
   169→            last_active = _relative_time(s.get("last_active"))
   170→            sid = s["id"][:18]
   171→
   172→            # Adaptive column widths based on terminal width
   173→            # Layout: [arrow 3] [title/preview flexible] [active 12] [src 6] [id 18]
   174→            fixed_cols = 3 + 12 + 6 + 18 + 6  # arrow + active + src + id + padding
   175→            name_width = max(20, max_x - fixed_cols)
   176→
   177→            if title:
   178→                name = title[:name_width]
   179→            elif preview:
   180→                name = preview[:name_width]
   181→            else:
   182→                name = sid
   183→
   184→            return f"{name:<{name_width}}  {last_active:<10}  {source:<5} {sid}"
   185→
   186→        def _match(s, query):
   187→            """Check if a session matches the search query (case-insensitive)."""
   188→            q = query.lower()
   189→            return (
   190→                q in (s.get("title") or "").lower()
   191→                or q in (s.get("preview") or "").lower()
   192→                or q in s.get("id", "").lower()
   193→                or q in (s.get("source") or "").lower()
   194→            )
   195→
   196→        def _curses_browse(stdscr):
   197→            curses.curs_set(0)
   198→            if curses.has_colors():
   199→                curses.start_color()
   200→                curses.use_default_colors()
